ParlaMint-PL_2019-06-12-sejm-82-1.u412	Mrs. Marshal! High Izbo! Today I am pleased to present a project commonly called an anti-zator project, about amending some laws, which is precisely aimed at reducing the problem of payment congestion in Poland. So maybe I'll start with a certain presentation of some basic data showing the scale of the problem, which somehow also justify why we decided to start working on the law on this problem. Thus, according to the market data, around 80 Polish companies declare problems with late payments. In the first quarter of 2019, the value of unpaid invoices in the portfolio of such an average company is on average 22.7% I'm counting in percentage, that's for every $1,000. PLN 227. The average waiting time for payment is 3 months. Over three months, I'm sorry. And let's add to this general picture also that the smaller the company that experiences congestion, the proportional this basket is bigger for it, i.e. for the smallest companies, for micro-entrepreneurs, for small companies the problem of congestion is much more painful. It's even obvious in its own way, isn't it? Large companies are companies that usually have large reserves and have cheaper access to credit. Over-terming, the non-payment of invoices by their debtors can usually be offset in this way. For a small company, a longer period of lack of cash, such as, say, failure to pay, probably even a few more important invoices, may involve serious problems, and in extreme cases even bankruptcy. It is also worth noting that we have EU regulation in this area. We have a Directive on payment dates in commercial transactions in 2011, which Poland has implemented, but such an EU ex post evaluation, OSR ex post, carried out at EU level, indicates that the solutions contained there have not been entirely successful. In general, the law provides for a whole range of such tools, but private ones, i.e. those where the creditor himself must show initiative, must, I don't know, go to court to enforce his rights. For very different reasons, this is not happening. At the same time, this directive is not, if any, closed. It allows Member States, individual Member States, to introduce solutions that are more beneficial to the creditor than those in the directive. Interestingly, individual countries benefit from this possibility. And so on. in the Netherlands, in such asymmetrical contracts between a large entrepreneur and an SME entrepreneur, large entities may not set a payment deadline of more than 60 days. In the UK, however, this is the obligation to report on the payment practices used. Each large company must publish information on its payment deadlines. In France, on the other hand, we have such maximum statutory limits, but in particular sectors, as well as administrative, administrative and non-private penalties, for companies that are excessively, say, slow to pay their commitments. And now we're seeing, collecting signals from the market about how big the problem is, we started work and started it not with the project that I'm presenting now, but with the Green Paper. But I would like to stress that because I would like to point out that this law was created in dialogue with entrepreneurs. In April 2018, we published just such a Green Paper, which, on the one hand, described the problem, and on the other, we described what it looks like in other European countries, what solutions are used in the European Union, and we consulted, we asked individual entrepreneurs, as well as business associations, to take the floor, whether they are against, whether this particular solution makes sense or not. We have gathered a response which I would sum up about this way: congestion is an area where even such entrepreneurs or business associations, which very often stand in such a position very, I would say free market, do not intervene, we will manage ourselves, in this area they see the meaning of state intervention, because they realise that despite private legal tools it is often so that particularly smaller, economically weaker and contract-based companies do not use these rights. The results of the consultation of the Green Paper and the legal and comparative studies have been completed by the project. As a result, a project was created, which was, of course, consulted, agreed, and which I am pleased to present to you at this moment. I would now like to recall some of the key solutions that we have here. This is a package project and therefore changes a number of other laws across the board. We are aware that the problem of payment congestion is a multifaceted, complex and certainly multi-cause problem. You can't handle it with just one tool or just one mechanism. Rather, we need to take advantage of the whole range of solutions and try to tackle this problem on many fronts. I think that such a flagship solution contained in the project is the maximum payment deadlines. According to philosophy: we start with each other, we provide for a 30-day payment deadline for a transaction in which on the one hand there will be a public entity and on the other hand - an entrepreneur. When a public entity is a debtor, it may not be on an invoice or in a contract with a longer than 30-day payment deadline. The maximum, 60-day term is proposed in the so-called. asymmetric relationships, i.e. when on the one hand we have SMEs and on the other hand a large entrepreneur as a debtor. It seems to us that these 60 days - although I realize, of course, that this is interference in freedom of contract - is a reasonable term. As I say, we have looked at what it looks like in other countries, we have looked at it in agreements, in the context of the Green Paper consultation and in the RDS. We also looked at what it looks like from an entrepreneur's perspective. It seems that this 60-day time limit as a maximum time limit is a reasonable time limit. There is also a reporting obligation in relation to large businesses, following the British solution that I mentioned earlier. It is about reports to the Minister responsible for the economic affairs of precisely applicable payment deadlines. I think it's on the one hand. It's a soft solution because it doesn't involve some kind of sanction. Nevertheless, this is a solution which I hope will be considered as an opportunity for some large companies to build a positive image, i.e. thanks to it you will be able to see how these companies treat their nearest business environment, their creditors, their counterparties, their stakeholders. I hope that this will allow us to build awareness that, as part of good practice, such a decent, reasonable payment term should also be considered as something to be boasted of as an asset in relations with other entrepreneurs. The most radical, farthest-reaching solution in the project is the solution modelled to some extent on French solutions. It's administrative, financial, public-law penalties. These punishments will be served by the president of the UOKiK. In this respect, the proposal provides for a new procedure, a new mandate for the President. These penalties will only threaten the largest, most unreliable debtors, for which the sum of late invoices will be PLN 2 million, not paid within 3 consecutive months. This solution is intended to apply only to extreme situations. I am aware that this is a far-reaching solution, but at both EU and national level there are very clear signals that purely private legal instruments are insufficient. Very often small companies do not enforce their rights to large debtors for various reasons. I think there's room for some state intervention here. Another important solution in the tax area, which I would like to point out further, is the so-called. relief for bad debts in CIT and PIT. This solution is modelled on the solution that we already have in the case of VAT. It means that if the invoice is not paid... Maybe otherwise, please forgive me. This is an exception to the principle of memorial, i.e. If someone has an invoice they've issued, they're basically gonna have to take it into their own hands. Someone issued the invoice, she's unpaid, but it's still accounted for as his income. That's how the memorial works. We want a creditor who is unsatisfied whose invoice has not been paid in contractual terms after 90 days to be able to reduce his income by that amount. It seems to us some injustice that on the one hand he did not receive the money, because the invoice was not paid, and on the other hand it is treated as income, so he will have to pay tax on it. A relief for bad debts is supposed to be such a mechanism, I would say, to mitigate this tax consequence associated with congestion. In the area of civil procedure, we have a solution that simplifies the procedure for obtaining collateral for commercial transactions with a value not exceeding 75 000. PLN, i.e. in the jurisdiction of regional courts. I realize that security is, of course, not the same as a judgment ruling, but the security has this advantage, that it can often be obtained quite quickly, and it actually depends most often, often, I say in some simplification, on freezing somewhere, in the account, money, which later is a chance to satisfy itself. This is, I think, an important advantage of this solution. Here, too, we have a solution that extends the catalogue of unfair competition to an action involving unjustified extension of payment deadlines for goods or services supplied. This is already such a classic private-law tool, but it seems to us also important in practice, it is about claims concerning dishonesty. Entrepreneurs use it. It seems to me that such an additional tool or, let's say, a weapon in this arsenal wouldn't hurt the creditor, and I'm counting on it to help. To conclude, I would like to say a few more sentences in general. I am aware of this and it is important to realise that payment congestion is a complex problem. It has many reasons. Many of them are not, let's say, purely legal issues, many of them are the question of access to capital, also of a culture, of many things. It's not a problem that can just be legally resolved in some way, like a cut. Nevertheless, it seems to me that it can be limited. But we are aware that here we are dealing with interference in the area of freedom of contract. Therefore, we have a declaration in the Act, in the OSR, sorry, concerning the annual, for 3 years of validity of the Act, an annual analysis of the effects of the regulation. This is very important and we take it really seriously. It seems to me that this will have to be analyzed, because in a few years, some of these solutions will work well, let them remain, some may have to be modified somehow. I think you have to look at it a little bit the way you look at it, I don't know, when you seal the tax system. This is not a process that can be done with one move. This is rather just spread over some steps and I would take it as a second but very important step - the second, because the first was probably the implementation of the EU directive. I'm asking you kindly to accept the project. Thank you. [[Applause]]
ParlaMint-PL_2019-06-12-sejm-82-1.u413	Thank you. The Sejm agreed that in the discussion on this item it would hear five minutes' statements on behalf of clubs and circles. I'm opening a discussion. Mrs Ewa Malik will be the first to speak on behalf of the Law and Justice Club. You're welcome.
ParlaMint-PL_2019-06-12-sejm-82-1.u414	Mrs. Marshal! Ladies and gentlemen! Minister! On behalf of the Parliamentary Club, Law and Justice, I have the honour to present an opinion on the Government's draft law amending certain laws in order to limit payment congestion, Parliamentary Printing No 3475. The aim of the project is to introduce solutions to mobilise traders' debtors, both public entities and traders, to pay within a fixed period of time, and to effectively discourage them from unduly extending their payment deadlines. The main objective of the Act is to improve the legal environment in which entrepreneurs operate, the main economic actors in Poland, which is enshrined in the most important document of the Government of Law and Justice, i.e. in the Strategy for Responsible Development. According to the European Payment Report, around 78% entrepreneurs receive payment for the work done 30 days after the deadline and a further 13% remains without payment up to 60 days. The analysis of the structure of outstanding claims showed that the average maturity of late repayment is the longest in the group of micro-entrepreneurs and is 3 months and 12 days, and in the group of large companies - 2 months and 21 days. Thus, the smaller the enterprise, the greater the share of the costs resulting from late payments in total costs. The government project calls for a reduced period of 30 days for payment in transactions in which a public entity is a debtor and without the possibility of extension. The only exception to this rule is medical establishments for which a 60-day settlement deadline is still proposed. Since public entities are usually a good and reliable payer, the amendment of the rules towards the legal prohibition of exceeding the 30-day payment deadline meets with a positive opinion among entrepreneurs. Directive 2011/7/EU of the European Parliament and of the Council on combating late payment in commercial transactions, which constitutes the basis of the Polish Act of 8 March 2013 on payment dates in commercial transactions, recognises the specific nature of medicinal entities in the need to ensure a balance between individual patient needs and the financial resources available to health institutions. The practice of setting excessively extended payment deadlines in contracts also contributes to the phenomenon of payment congestion to a large extent. The government project introduces a statutory obligation to apply a maximum 60-day payment deadline in transactions where the creditor is a small or medium-sized company and the debtor is a large trader. The largest companies will also be subject, preventively, to an additional reporting obligation consisting of the need to publish information on payment practices and payment deadlines. Other solutions to the bill aimed at eliminating payment congestion include provisions on the application of civil-law measures, namely: strengthening the creditor's powers in commercial transactions, higher compensation for small and medium-sized enterprises, shorter payment deadlines, financial penalties for companies which are notoriously abusing their economic advantage. In addition, an extension of the debt relief to income tax, including the obligation for debtors to increase the tax base by more than 120 days past due. The draft law is not contrary to European Union law. The draft law is also accompanied by a consultation report. The results of the consultation have been written down in the form of a Green Paper and confirm clearly that entrepreneurs are faced with the practice of late settlement by counterparties. [[♪ The bell ♪]] This is the main cause of difficulties in conducting business activity in Poland. To sum up, the changes adopted by the government in the law are intentional and are conducive to safeguarding both the interests of individual economic operators, especially small and medium-sized enterprises, and to strengthening fair competition in economic trade. The Law and Justice Club supports the provisions of the bill proposed by the government, the sejm print No 3475 and recommends its adoption in the Polish Sejm. Thank you very much. [[Applause]]

In less than 3 hours, a group of outstanding students, the winners of the first 5th Gorlicka Patriotic Song Competition and the winners of the two historical competitions will leave Gorlice for a trip to Warsaw. The historical competition for middle school students, and now the students of the last classes of primary schools - the Constitution on 3 May - times of Stanisławowski, which I have organised for 11 years, reminds young people of important events from the end of the 18th century. The Constitution adopted on 3 May 1791, i.e. The law governing the legal system of the Republic of Poland of both Nations was the first in Europe and the second in the world written law of this kind. This constitution was an attempt to radically heal the state and eliminate the influence of foreign powers. The result was a war in defence of the Constitution and unfortunately further partitions. Despite the partitions, the memory of the latter in the history of the world of the written national constitution has helped to sustain Polish aspirations for independence and the creation of a just society. Supporters of the Constitution on 3 May considered that the state and its independent existence were an overriding good, which also depended on the existence of citizens' freedom.
